Anthropic shares close below IPO price as AI jitters return

Category: AI

Anthropic shares closed Tuesday at $81.91, down 6.5% and below the $83 IPO price for the first time, just four trading days after the Claude developer’s record-setting Nasdaq debut [31][11]. The stock opened at $111.50 on Nov. 19 and fell as low as $76.60 during Tuesday’s session; Reuters said the decline put nearly $200 billion in market value at risk [31]. Investors are reassessing AI companies’ valuations and computing infrastructure costs, with higher interest rates adding to financing concerns [31][82]. Anthropic also missed Monday’s rally in AI shares after Nvidia reinstated Jensen Huang as chief executive. On Tuesday, the Nasdaq fell 1.5% and the S&P 500 lost 0.5%, while the Dow rose 0.9% [31][82]. Separately, Business Insider and MarketWatch reported that employees at Anthropic and rival OpenAI have questioned internally whether the companies can safely release their most powerful unreleased models [4][7]. Anthropic said it recently delayed pretraining a new frontier model for more than a month to strengthen security [7].

Primary Source: View original article

If we do see a slowdown in the data-center buildout, then that obviously can impact the speed at which Anthropic can develop and therefore future revenue and therefore its valuation

— Fiona Cincotta, senior market analyst at StoneX (News18 (Reuters))

Did you know: About 5,100 of Anthropic’s roughly 5,300 employees held equity stakes valued above $1 million after the first day of trading, according to Meduza [67].

Key Points

  • Options activity: Nearly 500,000 options contracts traded by noon Tuesday, carrying about $250 million in premiums; puts outnumbered calls roughly 300,000 to 200,000, while large trades suggested divided views [1].
  • AI model debate: The White House supports open-weight AI models, whose components can be downloaded and adapted. These models can broaden demand for Nvidia computing beyond a few leading model developers; Anthropic and OpenAI have questioned their implications [165].
  • Chip tracking: A bill promoted by Senate Intelligence Committee Chairman Tom Cotton would require chipmakers to track advanced AI processors and notify the government if they suspect chips have reached prohibited users [216].
  • Institutional allocation: About half of the institutional investors who placed orders for the IPO received no shares; those firms did not appear to be rushing to buy on the pullback [24].
  • IPO supply: The Financial Times reported that underwriters exercised the full greenshoe option after one trading day, raising another $12.2 billion, while 315 million non-founder insider shares were eligible for sale from day one [72].
  • Executive sale: A filing showed CEO Dario Amodei sold more than 3.37 million shares worth about $280 million to IPO underwriters as part of the offering, rather than on the open market [16].
  • Index inclusion: MSCI is scheduled to add Anthropic to its global standard indexes after the Nov. 30 close; one analysis estimated passive-fund demand could equal 30% of the IPO shares by year-end [74].

Perspectives

Wedbush Securities: Analyst Ygal Arounian rated the stock Outperform with a $120 target, arguing that frontier AI could serve the much larger knowledge-work market rather than just traditional software budgets. He estimated Anthropic’s market penetration at less than 1%.

Sources: CNBC

CFRA Research: Equity analyst Janice Quek maintained a Buy rating, saying customers continued to use Anthropic’s services despite cheaper AI products, while warning that growing competition from open-weight models remained a risk.

Sources: Cnyes

Arisa Liu, a research fellow at Taiwan Industry Economics Services, said Huang’s personal ties to Taiwan remove a perceived risk that a successor might weaken Nvidia’s relationship with the island’s chip supply chain [147].

Sources: South China Morning Post

Timeline

  • 19 November 2026: Anthropic began Nasdaq trading after pricing shares at $83 [31].
  • 23 November 2026: Nvidia reinstated Jensen Huang as chief executive, lifting AI shares [63][82].
  • 24 November 2026: Anthropic closed below its IPO price for the first time [31][11].
  • 30 November 2026: MSCI inclusion in global standard indexes is scheduled after the close [74].
  • 12 November 2026: Huang resigned after Nvidia’s board accepted his departure [242]
  • 23 November 2026: Nvidia’s board reinstated Huang as CEO and president [120]
  • 24 November 2026: Huang addressed employees at his first all-hands meeting since his return [14]
  • 1 December 2026: Huang is scheduled for closed-door interviews with three House committees [188]
  • 3 December 2026: Huang is scheduled to testify publicly before the Senate Banking Committee [227]

Industry Impact

  • Taiwan chip supply chain: Huang’s reinstatement helped lift Taiwan’s benchmark Taiex nearly 9%, while analysts said the island’s manufacturers could attract additional orders if US scrutiny redirects Nvidia business from mainland China [147].
  • AI infrastructure finance: Nvidia’s loans to and investments in major customers helped support the AI buildout, so leadership uncertainty raised concerns beyond its role as a chip supplier [149].

Historical Background

Reuters cited two prominent comparisons with Anthropic’s early drop below its offer price: SpaceX took about a month to fall below its IPO price [31]. Facebook, now Meta Platforms, fell below its offer price on its second trading day in 2012 [31].

Business Angle

  • Monitor whether Anthropic sustains its progress on free cash flow: Man Group said the company was already bringing in cash and was close to breaking even on free cash flow [86].
  • Track whether management can explain the slowdown in sequential revenue growth: Seaport Research’s Jay Goldberg cited a decline from 144% in the second quarter to 44% in the third [9].

Q&A

Q: What investigations involving Nvidia remain open?

A: Nvidia’s independent-director inquiry is continuing, the Justice Department is investigating alleged chip diversion through Megaspeed, and the SEC has reportedly examined the company’s accounting practices and shareholder communications [188].
